# The Noun Dictionary

> **13313 entries** across 101 categories.

**[Open the audio browser](browse.html)** to search all 13313 and hear any of them spoken.

## Categories

| # | Category | What it covers | Entries |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | [Plurals made by changing the vowel](01-vowel-change.md) | No ending at all — the vowel inside the word changes: foot → feet, mouse → mice. Seven nouns, and that is the entire list. | 8 |
| 2 | [The -en plurals and the odd ones left over](02-en-and-suppletive.md) | Child → children, ox → oxen, and a handful of nouns whose plural is a different word entirely: person → people, die → dice, penny → pence. | 6 |
| 3 | [Plurals that look exactly like the singular](03-unchanged.md) | One sheep, two sheep. No ending, no vowel change — only the surrounding words tell you which you mean. | 47 |
| 4 | [When -f becomes -ves](04-f-to-ves.md) | Knife → knives, leaf → leaves. The final /f/ voices to /v/ — but only for about two dozen words, and roof, chief, and belief are not among them. | 37 |
| 5 | [Nouns ending in -o](05-o-plurals.md) | Potato → potatoes, but photo → photos. The ending decides, and so does where the word came from. | 43 |
| 6 | [Latin -us → -i](06-latin-us-i.md) | Cactus → cacti, fungus → fungi. A real pattern with a short membership list — and virus, campus, and bonus are not in it. | 33 |
| 7 | [Latin -a → -ae](07-latin-a-ae.md) | Larva → larvae, vertebra → vertebrae. A small group, and for several of them the plain English -s has already won. | 22 |
| 8 | [Latin -um → -a](08-latin-um-a.md) | Datum → data, medium → media. The plural often ends up feeling like a singular — which is why data is and media is are now normal American English. | 30 |
| 9 | [Greek -is → -es](09-greek-is-es.md) | Analysis → analyses, crisis → crises. The spelling change is small; the sound change is not — the plural ends in /-siːz/, SEEZ. | 29 |
| 10 | [Greek -on → -a](10-greek-on-a.md) | Criterion → criteria, phenomenon → phenomena. A group of four, and two of them are among the most misused words in English. | 4 |
| 11 | [-ex and -ix → -ices](11-ex-ix-ices.md) | Index → indices, matrix → matrices. The technical plural and the everyday plural often coexist — and mean slightly different things. | 14 |
| 12 | [Plurals borrowed from other languages](12-other-foreign.md) | Graffito → graffiti, cherub → cherubim, bureau → bureaux. Italian, Hebrew, and French plurals that English kept. | 25 |

| 21 | [Uncountable nouns](13-uncountable.md) | ✗ an information, ✗ two advices, ✗ many furnitures. These nouns have no plural and take no a — you count them with a piece of, an item of, a bit of. | 92 |
| 22 | [Core vocabulary: words spelled alike, said differently](130-core-homographs.md) | One spelling, two pronunciations, two meanings — bass the fish and bass the low notes are different words that happen to look the same. | 21 |
| 23 | [Nouns that are countable and uncountable — with different meanings](14-dual-category.md) | A hair is one strand; hair is what grows on your head. A glass is a container; glass is a material. Same word, two grammars, two meanings. | 42 |
| 24 | [Plural-only nouns (pluralia tantum)](15-plural-only.md) | Scissors, jeans, clothes. Always plural, always a plural verb, and no singular exists — to count them you need a pair of. | 44 |
| 25 | [Nouns that end in -s but are singular](16-singular-with-s.md) | The news is good. Physics is hard. Measles is contagious. They look plural. They are not. | 28 |
| 26 | [Collective nouns](17-collective.md) | The team is winning in America; the team are winning in Britain. One noun, one group, two grammars — and police is plural everywhere. | 28 |
| 27 | [Plurals of compound nouns](18-compound-plurals.md) | Mothers-in-law, not ✗ mother-in-laws. Pluralize the noun that carries the meaning — which is usually not the last word. | 24 |
| 28 | [Partitive nouns — how to count the uncountable](19-partitive.md) | A piece of advice, a slice of bread, a bar of soap, a pinch of salt. The uncountable noun cannot take a number, so a countable one carries it. | 31 |
| 29 | [Nationality nouns](20-nationality.md) | Two Americans, two Japanese, two Spaniards — but never ✗ two Japaneses and never ✗ two Englishes. Four endings, four different grammars. | 30 |
| 30 | [Collective nouns for animals](21-terms-of-venery.md) | A pride of lions, a murder of crows, a parliament of owls. English has a specific collective noun for almost every animal, and using the wrong one is instantly noticeable. | 35 |
| 31 | [Nouns that come in gender pairs](22-gender-pairs.md) | Actor/actress, waiter/waitress, widow/widower. Some pairs are alive, some are dying, and using a dying one marks you as out of date. | 20 |
| 32 | [Proper nouns, brand names, and words that lost their capital](23-proper-and-brand.md) | Aspirin, escalator, and zipper were once trademarks. Kleenex and Xerox still are. The capital letter is the whole grammar here. | 22 |
